Spencer was disappointed at his lack of excitement. His news did not seem to interest him.

Kennedy went back to the football field to inform Jimmy Silver of the result of his investigations.

===Chinese===
「哦，」肯尼迪不动声色地说，「沃尔顿？你碰见他了？谢谢。」

斯宾塞见他毫无兴奋之意，大失所望。他这条新闻似乎没能引起对方半点兴趣。

肯尼迪回到橄榄球场，把调查结果通报给吉米·西尔弗。

## XII Kennedy Interviews Walton / 第十二章 肯尼迪约谈沃尔顿

===Original===
“I’m very sorry,” he said, when he rejoined the shivering group, “but I’m afraid we shall have to call this match off. There seems to have been a mistake. None of my team are anywhere about. I’m awfully sorry, sir,” he added, to Mr. Blackburn, “to have given you all this trouble for nothing.”

“Not at all, Kennedy. We must try another day.”

Mr. Blackburn suspected that something untoward had happened in Kay’s to cause this sudden defection of the first fifteen of the house. He knew that Kennedy was having a hard time in his new position, and he did not wish to add to his discomfort by calling for an explanation before an audience. It could not be pleasant for Kennedy to feel that his enemies had scored off him. It was best to preserve a discreet silence with regard to the whole affair, and leave him to settle it for himself.

===Chinese===
「非常抱歉，」他回到那群瑟瑟发抖的人中间时说，「恐怕这场比赛只好取消了。似乎是出了什么岔子。我的队员一个都不见踪影。实在对不住，先生，」他又对布莱克本先生说，「害您白辛苦一场。」

「哪里的话，肯尼迪。咱们改天再赛就是。」

布莱克本先生猜到凯氏出了什么变故，才导致宿舍一队突然集体撂了挑子。他知道肯尼迪在新职位上日子艰难，不愿当着众人索要解释、雪上加霜。让肯尼迪觉得敌人讨了便宜，这滋味不会好受。对整件事还是识趣地保持沉默为妙，留他自己去解决。

===Original===
Jimmy Silver was more curious. He took Kennedy off to tea in his study, sat him down in the best chair in front of the fire, and proceeded to urge him to confess everything.

“Now, then, what’s it all about?” he asked, briskly, spearing a muffin on the fork and beginning to toast.

“It’s no good asking me,” said Kennedy. “I suppose it’s a put-up job to make me look a fool. I ought to have known something of this kind would happen when I saw what they did to my first notice.”

“What was that?”

Kennedy explained.

===Chinese===
吉米·西尔弗好奇心更重。他把肯尼迪带回自己书房喝茶，按进火炉前最好的椅子里，随即开始劝他招供一切。

「好了，到底是怎么回事？」他麻利地问，一边用叉子叉起一只玛芬饼，凑到火上烤。

「问我也是白问。」肯尼迪说，「我看这就是个做好了的局，存心叫我出丑。看到他们对我第一张布告下的手，我早该如何道会有这一出。」

「那是怎么回事？」

肯尼迪说了原委。

===Original===
“This is getting thrilling,” said Jimmy. “Just pass that plate. Thanks. What are you going to do about it?”

“I don’t know. What would you do?”

“My dear chap, I’d first find out who was at the bottom of it﻿—there’s bound to be one man who started the whole thing﻿—and I’d make it my aim in life to give him the warmest ten minutes he’d ever had.”

“That sounds all right. But how would you set about it?”

“Why, touch him up, of course. What else would you do? Before the whole house, too.”

“Supposing he wouldn’t be touched up?”

“Wouldn’t *be*! He’d have to.”

===Chinese===
「越来越刺激了。」吉米说，「把那个盘子递给我。谢谢。你打算怎么办？」

「不知道。换你怎么办？」

「老兄，我头一件事就是查出幕后主使——这种事必有一个牵头的人——然后把他此生最烫手的十分钟，立为我毕生的志向。」

「听着不错。可你怎么下手呢？」

「还用问，当然是『修理』他。除此之外还能干什么？还得当着全宿舍的面。」

「他要是不肯受『修理』呢？」

「不肯*受*！由不得他。」

===Original===
“You don’t know Kay’s, Jimmy. You’re thinking what you’d do if this had happened in Blackburn’s. The two things aren’t the same. Here the man would probably take it like a lamb. The feeling of the house would be against him. He’d find nobody to back him up. That’s because Blackburn’s is a decent house instead of being a sink like Kay’s. If I tried the touching-up before the whole house game with our chaps, the man would probably reply by going for me, assisted by the whole strength of the company.”

“Well, dash it all then, all you’ve got to do is to call a prefects’ meeting, and he’ll get ten times worse beans from them than he’d have got from you. It’s simple.”

Kennedy stared into the fire pensively.

“I don’t know,” he said. “I bar that prefects’ meeting business. It always seems rather feeble to me, lugging in a lot of chaps to help settle someone you can’t manage yourself. I want to carry this job through on my own.”

===Chinese===
「你不了解凯氏，吉米。你这是在盘算这事若出在布莱克本宿舍你会怎么办。两码事。在这儿，那人多半会乖乖受着。宿舍的舆论会压着他，他找不着一个撑腰的。那是因为布莱克本宿舍是个体面地方，不像凯氏是个烂泥坑。我要是跟咱们这帮人玩当众『修理』那一套，那人多半会扑上来跟我拼命，还要全屋好汉合力相助。」

「那好，见鬼，你只需召集一个级长会，他挨的揍会比挨你的狠上十倍。再简单不过。」

肯尼迪若有所思地盯着炉火。

「不好说。」他说，「我不喜欢级长会那一套。拉上一帮人来帮着收拾一个你自己收拾不了的人，在我看总是窝囊。这件事我要一个人办到底。」

===Original===
“Then you’d better scrap with the man.”

“I think I will.”

Silver stared.

“Don’t be an ass,” he said. “I was only rotting. You can’t go fighting all over the shop as if you were a fag. You’d lose your prefect’s cap if it came out.”

“I could wear my topper,” said Kennedy, with a grin. “You see,” he added, “I’ve not much choice. I must do something. If I took no notice of this business there’d be no holding the house. I should be ragged to death. It’s no good talking about it. Personally, I should prefer touching the chap up to fighting him, and I shall try it on. But he’s not likely to meet me halfway. And if he doesn’t there’ll be an interesting turn-up, and you shall hold the watch. I’ll send a kid round to fetch you when things look like starting. I must go now to interview my missing men. So long. Mind you slip round directly I send for you.”

“Wait a second. Don’t be in such a beastly hurry. Who’s the chap you’re going to fight?”

“I don’t know yet. Walton, I should think. But I don’t know.”

“Walton! By Jove, it’ll be worth seeing, anyhow, if we *are* both sacked for it when the Old Man finds out.”

===Chinese===
「那你最好跟那人干一架。」

「我看就这么办。」

西尔弗瞪大了眼。

「别犯傻。」他说，「我不过是说着玩的。你可不能像个跑腿生似的满世界打架。这事一捅出去，你的级长帽子就没了。」

「我可以改戴大礼帽嘛。」肯尼迪咧嘴一笑。「再说，」他补了一句，「我也没什么可选的。我总得做点什么。这事我要是不闻不问，宿舍就再没法管了，我会被作弄到死。多说无益。就我个人而言，比起打架，我更想『修理』那小子一顿，我会试上一试。可他多半不会俯首帖耳。他要是不从，那就有一场好架可看，你来掌表。看架势要开场时，我派个小子去叫你。我现在得去会会我那批失踪的队员了。回见。记住，我一叫你，你立马就得过来。」

「等一等。别这么火急火燎的。你要跟谁打？」

「还不知道。我想是沃尔顿。但拿不准。」

「沃尔顿！老天，且不说老头子查出来后咱们会不会双双被开除，单这一架就值看了。」

===Original===
Kennedy returned to his study and changed his football boots for a pair of gymnasium shoes. For the job he had in hand it was necessary that he should move quickly, and football boots are a nuisance on a board floor. When he had changed, he called Spencer.

“Go down to the senior dayroom,” he said, “and tell MacPherson I want to see him.”

MacPherson was a long, weak-looking youth. He had been put down to play for the house that day, and had not appeared.

“MacPherson!” said the fag, in a tone of astonishment, “not Walton?”

He had been looking forward to the meeting between Kennedy and his ancient foe, and to have a miserable being like MacPherson offered as a substitute disgusted him.

“If you have no objection,” said Kennedy, politely, “I may want you to fetch Walton later on.”

Spencer vanished, hopeful once more.

===Chinese===
肯尼迪回到书房，把橄榄球鞋换成一双体操鞋。手头这桩差事需要他脚下利索，而橄榄球鞋踩在木地板上纯是累赘。换好鞋，他把斯宾塞叫来。

「去高年级活动室，」他说，「告诉麦克弗森（MacPherson），我要见他。」

麦克弗森是个瘦长孱弱的少年。那天他被排进宿舍出赛名单，却没有露面。

「麦克弗森！」跑腿生惊讶出声，「不是沃尔顿？」

他一直盼着肯尼迪同宿敌的那场会面，如今端上来一个麦克弗森这样的可怜虫顶数，只叫他倒胃口。

「你要是不反对的话，」肯尼迪彬彬有礼地说，「过会儿可能还要劳你去请沃尔顿。」

斯宾塞一溜烟跑了，心里重新燃起希望。

===Original===
“Come in, MacPherson,” said Kennedy, on the arrival of the long one; “shut the door.”

MacPherson did so, feeling as if he were paying a visit to the dentist. As long as there had been others with him in this affair he had looked on it as a splendid idea. But to be singled out like this was quite a different thing.

“Now,” said Kennedy, “why weren’t you on the field this afternoon?”

“I﻿—er﻿—I was kept in.”

“How long?”

“Oh﻿—er﻿—till about five.”

“What do you call about five?”

“About twenty-five to,” he replied, despondently.

“Now look here,” said Kennedy, briskly, “I’m just going to explain to you exactly how I stand in this business, so you’d better attend. I didn’t ask to be made head of this sewage depot. If I could have had any choice, I wouldn’t have touched a Kayite with a barge-pole. But since I am head, I’m going to be it, and the sooner you and your senior dayroom crew realise it the better. This sort of thing isn’t going on. I want to know now who it was put up this job. You wouldn’t have the cheek to start a thing like this yourself. Who was it?”

===Chinese===
「进来，麦克弗森。」瘦长条一到，肯尼迪便说，「把门关上。」

麦克弗森照办了，那感觉仿佛是去看牙医。这件事只要有别人陪着一起扛，他一直觉得是个妙主意。可像这样被单拎出来，就完全是另一回事了。

「说吧，」肯尼迪说，「今天下午你为什么没到场？」

「我——呃——我被留堂了。」

「留到几点？」

「哦——呃——到五点左右。」

「你说的五点左右是几点？」

「差不多差二十五分五点。」他垂头丧气地答道。

「现在你听好，」肯尼迪干脆利落地说，「我这就把我在这件事里的立场原原本本讲给你听，所以你最好竖起耳朵。没人求着来当这个化粪池的舍长。要是由我挑，我连凯氏人拿船篙都不想碰。可我既然是舍长，就要当出个舍长样，你和你那高年级活动室的狐群狗党越早明白这一点越好。这种事不能再继续了。我现在要知道，这局是谁做的。你还没那个胆子自己起这么一个头。是谁？」

===Original===
“Well﻿—er﻿—”

“You’d better say, and be quick, too. I can’t wait. Whoever it was. I shan’t tell him you told me. And I shan’t tell Kay. So now you can go ahead. Who was it?”

“Well﻿—er﻿—Walton.”

“I thought so. Now you can get out. If you see Spencer, send him here.”

Spencer, curiously enough, was just outside the door. So close to it, indeed, that he almost tumbled in when MacPherson opened it.

“Go and fetch Walton,” said Kennedy.

===Chinese===
「这个——呃——」

「你最好说出来，还要快。我等不了。不管是谁。我不会告诉他说是你说的。我也不告诉凯。所以你现在可以放心讲了。是谁？」

「这个——呃——沃尔顿。」

「我就知道。你可以滚了。要是看见斯宾塞，叫他到这儿来。」

说来也巧，斯宾塞就在门外，而且贴得极近，麦克弗森一开门，他险些跌进来。

「去把沃尔顿叫来。」肯尼迪说。

===Original===
Spencer dashed off delightedly, and in a couple of minutes Walton appeared. He walked in with an air of subdued defiance, and slammed the door.

“Don’t bang the door like that,” said Kennedy. “Why didn’t you turn out today?”

“I was kept in.”

“Couldn’t you get out in time to play?”

“No.”

“When did you get out?”

“Six.”

“Not before?”

“I said six.”

===Chinese===
斯宾塞欢天喜地地飞奔而去，两分钟后沃尔顿驾到。他走进屋来，带着一股压着火的挑衅劲儿，砰地摔上门。

「别这么摔门。」肯尼迪说，「今天你为什么不出场？」

「我被留堂了。」

「不能及时脱身来比赛吗？」

「不能。」

「你几点脱身的？」

「六点。」

「没更早？」

「我说了，六点。」

===Original===
“Then how did you manage to go down town﻿—without leave, by the way, but that’s a detail﻿—at half-past five?”

“All right,” said Walton; “better call me a liar.”

“Good suggestion,” said Kennedy, cheerfully; “I will.”

“It’s all very well,” said Walton. “You know jolly well you can say anything you like. I can’t do anything to you. You’d have me up before the prefects.”

“Not a bit of it. This is a private affair between ourselves. I’m not going to drag the prefects into it. You seem to want to make this house worse than it is. I want to make it more or less decent. We can’t both have what we want.”

There was a pause.

“When would it be convenient for you to be touched up before the whole house?” inquired Kennedy, pleasantly.

“What?”

“Well, you see, it seems the only thing. I must take it out of someone for this house-match business, and you started it. Will tonight suit you, after supper?”

===Chinese===
「那你五点半是怎么进的城——顺便说一句，还是没请假出的界，不过这是小事——？」

「行啊，」沃尔顿说，「干脆说我是骗子得了。」

「好提议，」肯尼迪欣然道，「我就这么说。」

「你说得倒轻巧。」沃尔顿说，「你清楚得很，你想说什么都行。我拿你没办法。你会把我扭送级长会。」

「一点也不会。这是咱们两人之间的私事。我不打算把级长们扯进来。你好像巴不得这宿舍比现在更烂。我想让它多少像点样子。咱们俩的愿望，不能都得逞。」

一时无话。

「你什么时候方便接受当着全宿舍的『修理』？」肯尼迪和颜悦色地问。

「什么？」

「呃，你看，看来只有这一条路了。宿舍赛这笔账我总得找个人出，而你是带头的。今晚如何，晚饭之后合你意吗？」

===Original===
“You’ll get it hot if you try to touch me.”

“We’ll see.”

“You’d funk taking me on in a scrap,” said Walton.

“Would I? As a matter of fact, a scrap would suit me just as well. Better. Are you ready now?”

“Quite, thanks,” sneered Walton. “I’ve knocked you out before, and I’ll do it again.”

“Oh, then it was you that night at camp? I thought so. I spotted your style. Hitting a chap when he wasn’t ready, you know, and so on. Now, if you’ll wait a minute, I’ll send across to Blackburn’s for Silver. I told him I should probably want him as a timekeeper tonight.”

“What do you want with Silver. Why won’t Perry do?”

“Thanks, I’m afraid Perry’s timekeeping wouldn’t be impartial enough. Silver, I think, if you don’t mind.”

===Chinese===
「你敢动我一根指头，有你好受的。」

「走着瞧。」

「真要动手，你准怂。」沃尔顿说。

「我会吗？说实话，打一架对我正合适。更合适。你现在就准备好了？」

「好得很，多谢关心。」沃尔顿冷笑，「我以前揍趴下过你，这回照旧。」

「哦，那么营地里那天晚上果然是你？我料到是。我认出了你的路数。专挑人家没防备的时候下手，如此等等。你稍等片刻，我派人去布莱克本宿舍叫西尔弗。我跟他讲过，今晚多半要请他当计时员。」

「你要西尔弗干什么。佩里怎么不行？」

「多谢，恐怕佩里计时不够公正。还是西尔弗吧，你不介意吧。」

===Original===
Spencer was summoned once more, and despatched to Blackburn’s. He returned with Jimmy.

“Come in, Jimmy,” said Kennedy. “Run away, Spencer. Walton and I are just going to settle a point of order which has arisen, Jimmy. Will you hold the watch? We ought just to have time before tea.”

“Where?” asked Silver.

“My dormitory would be the best place. We can move the beds. I’ll go and get the keys.”

Kennedy’s dormitory was the largest in the house. After the beds had been moved back, there was a space in the middle of fifteen feet one way, and twelve the other﻿—not a large ring, but large enough for two fighters who meant business.

Walton took off his coat, waistcoat, and shirt. Kennedy, who was still in football clothes, removed his blazer.

“Half a second,” said Jimmy Silver﻿—“what length rounds?”

“Two minutes?” said Kennedy to Walton.

“All right,” growled Walton.

“Two minutes, then, and half a minute in between.”

“Are you both ready?” asked Jimmy, from his seat on the chest of drawers.

Kennedy and Walton advanced into the middle of the impromptu ring.

There was dead silence for a moment.

“Time!” said Jimmy Silver.

===Chinese===
斯宾塞又被唤来，派往布莱克本宿舍，回来时带着吉米。

「进来，吉米。」肯尼迪说，「斯宾塞，你去吧。沃尔顿和我正要解决一个刚冒出来的程序问题，吉米。你来掌表如何？赶在喝茶前应该正好来得及。」

「在哪儿打？」西尔弗问。

「我的寝室最合适。床可以搬开。我去拿钥匙。」

肯尼迪的寝室是全宿舍最大的一间。床搬开之后，中间腾出一块场地，一边十五英尺，一边十二英尺——拳台算不上大，但对两个动真格的来说尽够了。

沃尔顿脱掉外衣、马甲和衬衫。肯尼迪还穿着橄榄球服，只脱了运动夹克。

「慢着，」吉米·西尔弗说，「一回合多长？」

「两分钟？」肯尼迪问沃尔顿。

「行。」沃尔顿咕哝道。

「那就两分钟一回合，中间休息半分钟。」

「两位都准备好了？」吉米在五斗柜上的座位里问。

肯尼迪和沃尔顿走进临时拳台的中央。

一时间死一般的寂静。

「时间到！」吉米·西尔弗喊道。

## XIII The Fight in the Dormitory / 第十三章 寝室之战

===Original===
Stating it broadly, fighters may be said to be divided into two classes﻿—those who are content to take two blows if they can give three in return, and those who prefer to receive as little punishment as possible, even at the expense of scoring fewer points themselves. Kennedy’s position, when Jimmy Silver called time, was peculiar. On all the other occasions on which he had fought﻿—with the gloves on in the annual competition, and at the assault-at-arms﻿—he had gone in for the policy of taking all that the other man liked to give him, and giving rather more in exchange. Now, however, he was obliged to alter his whole style. For a variety of reasons it was necessary that he should come out of this fight with as few marks as possible. To begin with, he represented, in a sense, the Majesty of the Law. He was tackling Walton more by way of an object-lesson to the Kayite mutineers than for his own personal satisfaction. The object-lesson would lose in impressiveness if he were compelled to go about for a week or so with a pair of black eyes, or other adornments of a similar kind. Again﻿—and this was even more important﻿—if he was badly marked the affair must come to the knowledge of the headmaster. Being a prefect, and in the sixth form, he came into contact with the Head every day, and the disclosure of the fact that he had been engaged in a pitched battle with a member of his house, who was, in addition to other disadvantages, very low down in the school, would be likely to lead to unpleasantness. A school prefect of Eckleton was supposed to be hedged about with so much dignity that he could quell turbulent inferiors with a glance. The idea of one of the august body lowering himself to the extent of emphasising his authority with the bare knuckle would scandalise the powers.

===Chinese===
大而化之地讲，拳手可以说分两类：一类乐于挨两拳换三拳，另一类宁可自己少得分，也巴不得少受些皮肉之苦。吉米·西尔弗喊出「时间到」的那一刻，肯尼迪的处境颇为特殊。以往凡有交手——戴拳套的年度大赛，还有武技表演赛——他奉行的方针都是对方愿意给多少就照单全收，回敬时再多添几分。如今却不得不改弦更张，整套打法都得变。出于种种缘由，这一架他必须尽量少带痕迹地走出来。首先，从某种意义上说，他代表着法律的威严。他收拾沃尔顿，与其说图自己痛快，不如说是给凯氏叛军上一堂示范课。若是他被迫顶着两只乌青眼或诸如此类的饰物满校园转悠一星期，这堂示范课的说服力可就要打折了。再者——这一层更要紧——他要是挂了彩，事情势必传到校长耳朵里。他是级长，又是六年级生，天天同校长照面。一旦露出风声，说他同本宿舍一个学生真刀真枪干了一场——那学生除了别的短处之外，在全校还是个辈分极低的角色——多半要生出麻烦来。在埃克尔顿，校级级长理应被重重威严包裹着，看低年级顽童一眼就能镇住。这样一位体面人物竟自贬身价、要靠赤手空拳来强调权威，此念一出，足叫当道诸公哗然。

===Original===
So Kennedy, rising at the call of time from the bed on which he sat, came up to the scratch warily.

Walton, on the other hand, having everything to gain and nothing to lose, and happy in the knowledge that no amount of bruises could do him any harm, except physically, came on with the evident intention of making a hurricane fight of it. He had very little science as a boxer. Heavy two-handed slogging was his forte, and, as the majority of his opponents up to the present had not had sufficient skill to discount his strength, he had found this a very successful line of action. Kennedy and he had never had the gloves on together. In the competition of the previous year both had entered in their respective classes, Kennedy as a lightweight, Walton in the middles, and both, after reaching the semifinal, had been defeated by the narrowest of margins by men who had since left the school. That had been in the previous Easter term, and, while Walton had remained much the same as regards weight and strength, Kennedy, owing to a term of hard bowling and a summer holiday spent in the open, had filled out. They were now practically on an equality, as far as weight was concerned. As for condition, that was all in favour of Kennedy. He played football in his spare time. Walton, on the days when football was not compulsory, smoked cigarettes.

===Chinese===
于是，喊声响起，肯尼迪从坐着的床上一跃而起，步步提防地上了开打线。

沃尔顿则不然，他有得赚没得赔，又深知淤青再多也伤不着他——除了皮肉——于是摆明了要打一场疾风骤雨式的猛攻。论拳击技术，他没多少门道。双手重锤乱抡是他的看家本领，而迄今为止他的多数对手技艺不足以抵消他的蛮力，他自然觉得这条路子百试百灵。肯尼迪同他从没戴拳套交过手。去年的比赛，两人各报各级：肯尼迪打轻量级，沃尔顿打中量级，双双杀进半决赛，又都只以毫厘之差败给了后来离校的对手。那是上个复活节学期的事。沃尔顿的体重气力一仍其旧，肯尼迪却经过一学期苦练投球、一个露天度过的暑假，长壮实了。论体重，两人眼下实打实旗鼓相当。论状态，则全在肯尼迪一边：闲暇时他踢橄榄球；橄榄球不是强制项目的日子，沃尔顿抽香烟。

===Original===
Neither of the pair showed any desire to open the fight by shaking hands. This was not a friendly spar. It was business. The first move was made by Walton, who feinted with his right and dashed in to fight at close quarters. It was not a convincing feint. At any rate, it did not deceive Kennedy. He countered with his left, and swung his right at the body with all the force he could put into the hit. Walton went back a pace, sparred for a moment, then came in again, hitting heavily. Kennedy’s counter missed its mark this time. He just stopped a round sweep of Walton’s right, ducked to avoid a similar effort of his left, and they came together in a clinch.

In a properly regulated glove-fight, the referee, on observing the principals clinch, says, “Break away there, break away,” in a sad, reproachful voice, and the fighters separate without demur, being very much alive to the fact that, as far as that contest is concerned, their destinies are in his hands, and that any bad behaviour in the ring will lose them the victory. But in an impromptu turn-up like this one, the combatants show a tendency to ignore the rules so carefully mapped out by the present Marquess of Queensberry’s grandfather, and revert to the conditions of warfare under which Cribb and Spring won their battles. Kennedy and Walton, having clinched, proceeded to wrestle up and down the room, while Jimmy Silver looked on from his eminence in pained surprise at the sight of two men, who knew the rules of the ring, so far forgetting themselves.

To do Kennedy justice, it was not his fault. He was only acting in self-defence. Walton had started the hugging. Also, he had got the under-grip, which, when neither man knows a great deal of the science of wrestling, generally means victory. Kennedy was quite sure that he could not throw his antagonist, but he hung on in the knowledge that the round must be over shortly, when Walton would have to loose him.

===Chinese===
两人都没表现出要先握手再开打的意思。这不是友谊切磋，是办正事。先动手的是沃尔顿，右手虚晃一记，直冲上来打贴身。这虚招不大高明，至少骗不过肯尼迪。他左手还击，右手抡足了力气朝对方躯体猛砸。沃尔顿退后一步，虚应了几招，又压上来猛打。这回肯尼迪的还击落了空。他堪堪架住沃尔顿右手横扫过来的一记，又一低头躲开左手如法炮制的一记，两人扭抱在了一处。

在一场规规矩矩的拳套赛中，裁判一见两位主角扭抱，便会用沉痛而责备的嗓音说「分开，分开」，拳手们也毫无二话地分开，因为他们对如下事实了然于胸：就那场比赛而言，生死操在裁判手里，台上任何失礼都足以叫他们输掉胜利。可在这样一场临时起意的混斗里，交手双方往往把当今昆斯伯里侯爵的祖父精心规划的规则抛诸脑后，回归克里布与斯普林当年打天下的战争法则。肯尼迪和沃尔顿一经扭抱，就在屋里摔起跤来，上上下下搅作一团，吉米·西尔弗在高处看着，又痛心又惊讶：两个懂拳台规矩的人，竟会忘形到这般地步。

平心而论，这不能怪肯尼迪。他纯属自卫，是沃尔顿先搂上来的。而且沃尔顿抢到了下把位——两人摔跤都不甚高明时，这一般就意味着胜利。肯尼迪很清楚自己摔不倒对手，可他死死撑住，因为他知道回合马上就到头，届时沃尔顿不得不撒手。

===Original===
“Time,” said Jimmy Silver.

Kennedy instantly relaxed his grip, and in that instant Walton swung him off his feet, and they came down together with a crash that shook the room. Kennedy was underneath, and, as he fell, his head came into violent contact with the iron support of a bed.

Jimmy Silver sprang down from his seat.

“What are you playing at, Walton? Didn’t you hear me call time? It was a beastly foul﻿—the worst I ever saw. You ought to be sacked for a thing like that. Look here, Kennedy, you needn’t go on. I disqualify Walton for fouling.”

The usually genial James stammered with righteous indignation.

===Chinese===
「时间到。」吉米·西尔弗喊道。

肯尼迪立刻松了手，就趁这一瞬，沃尔顿把他抡离了地面，两人轰然倒地，震得屋子直颤。肯尼迪压在下面，倒下时脑袋重重磕在一张床的铁床架上。

吉米·西尔弗从座位上跳了下来。

「你搞什么名堂，沃尔顿？没听见我喊时间到吗？卑鄙的犯规——我平生仅见。这种事够你被开除的了。听我说，肯尼迪，你不必再打了。我判沃尔顿犯规告负。」

一向和气的詹姆斯正气得义愤填膺，说话都结巴了。

===Original===
Kennedy sat down on a bed, dizzily.

“No,” he said; “I’m going on.”

“But he fouled you.”

“I don’t care. I’ll look after myself. Is it time yet?”

“Ten seconds more, if you really are going on.”

He climbed back on to the chest of drawers.

“Time.”

===Chinese===
肯尼迪晕乎乎地在一张床上坐下。

「不，」他说，「我接着打。」

「可他犯规了。」

「我不管。我自己会当心。到时间了吗？」

「你要真接着打，还有十秒。」

他爬回五斗柜上。

「时间到。」

===Original===
Kennedy came up feeling weak and sick. The force with which he had hit his head on the iron had left him dazed.

Walton rushed in as before. He had no chivalrous desire to spare his man by way of compensation for fouling him. What monopolised his attention was the evident fact that Kennedy was in a bad way, and that a little strenuous infighting might end the affair in the desired manner.

It was at this point that Kennedy had reason to congratulate himself on donning gymnasium shoes. They gave him that extra touch of lightness which enabled him to dodge blows which he was too weak to parry. Everything was vague and unreal to him. He seemed to be looking on at a fight between Walton and some stranger.

Then the effect of his fall began to wear off. He could feel himself growing stronger. Little by little his head cleared, and he began once more to take a personal interest in the battle. It is astonishing what a power a boxer, who has learnt the art carefully, has of automatic fighting. The expert gentleman who fights under the pseudonym of “Kid M’Coy” once informed the present writer that in one of his fights he was knocked down by such a severe hit that he remembered nothing further, and it was only on reading the paper next morning that he found, to his surprise, that he had fought four more rounds after the blow, and won the battle handsomely on points. Much the same thing happened to Kennedy. For the greater part of the second round he fought without knowing it. When Jimmy Silver called time he was in as good case as ever, and the only effects of the blow on his head were a vast lump underneath the hair, and a settled determination to win or perish. In a few minutes the bell would ring for tea, and all his efforts would end in nothing. It was no good fighting a draw with Walton if he meant to impress the house. He knew exactly what Rumour, assisted by Walton, would make of the affair in that case. “Have you heard the latest?” A would ask of B. “Why, Kennedy tried to touch Walton up for not playing footer, and Walton went for him and would have given him frightful beans, only they had to go down to tea.” There must be none of that sort of thing.

===Chinese===
肯尼迪走上前来，只觉虚弱欲呕。脑袋撞上铁架的那股劲道，把他撞得发懵。

沃尔顿照旧猛冲上来。他可没有半分侠义心肠，想为方才的犯规赔个不是而对对手手下留情。占据他全部心神的明摆着是：肯尼迪不成了，再加一把近身猛攻，事情就能照着如意的方式了结。

正是在这时，肯尼迪有理由庆幸自己换了体操鞋。鞋底那一点额外的轻灵，让他躲开了那些他已虚弱得招架不住的拳头。周遭一切在他眼里虚虚实实。他仿佛是在旁观沃尔顿同一个陌生人打架。

摔跌的效应这时开始消退。他觉出自己气力渐回。脑子一点点清明起来，他重新开始亲自关心这场战事。一个精心学过这门艺术的拳手，其「自动运转」的能力之强，实在惊人。那位以「小子」麦科伊（Kid M’Coy）为艺名出赛的高人，曾告诉过本书作者：有一场他挨了狠狠一记被打倒，之后的事全不记得，直到次晨读报才惊讶地发现，中拳之后他又打了四个回合，还以点数漂亮取胜。肯尼迪的遭遇大同小异。第二回合的大半时间，他都是不知不觉打完的。吉米·西尔弗喊时间到时，他状态一如平素，脑袋上那一击只留下两样东西：头发底下一座大包，以及不胜即死的决心。再过几分钟，喝茶的铃就要响了，他的一切辛苦将付诸流水。他要想镇住宿舍，同沃尔顿打成平手毫无用处。那种情形下，流言再由沃尔顿添油加醋会传成什么样，他了然于胸。「听见最新消息了吗？」甲会问乙。「可不是嘛，肯尼迪因为沃尔顿不踢球要去『修理』他，沃尔顿反扑，差点没把他打个半死，可惜俩人得上楼喝茶去了。」这种话头可一个字都不能有。

===Original===
“Time,” said Jimmy Silver, breaking in on his meditations.

It was probably the suddenness and unexpectedness of it that took Walton aback. Up till now his antagonist had been fighting strictly on the defensive, and was obviously desirous of escaping punishment as far as might be possible. And then the fall at the end of round one had shaken him up, so that he could hardly fight at all at their second meeting. Walton naturally expected that it would be left to him to do the leading in round three. Instead of this, however, Kennedy opened the round with such a lightning attack that Walton was all abroad in a moment. In his most scientific mood he had never had the remotest notion of how to guard. He was aggressive and nothing else. Attacked by a quick hitter, he was useless. Three times Kennedy got through his guard with his left. The third hit staggered him. Before he could recover, Kennedy had got his right in, and down went Walton in a heap.

He was up again as soon as he touched the boards, and down again almost as soon as he was up. Kennedy was always a straight hitter, and now a combination of good cause and bad temper﻿—for the thought of the foul in the first round had stirred what was normally a more or less placid nature into extreme viciousness﻿—lent a vigour to his left arm to which he had hitherto been a stranger. He did not use his right again. It was not needed.

===Chinese===
「时间到。」吉米·西尔弗的喊声打断了他的沉思。

打了他一个措手不及的，多半正是这记突袭的又快又险。到目前为止，对手一直严守守势，明摆着一心只想能少挨则少挨。而第一回合末尾那一摔又把他震得不轻，二次照面时几乎没了招架之力。沃尔顿自然料定，第三回合还得由他来主导。岂料肯尼迪开场便是一轮闪电般的抢攻，沃尔顿顷刻间方寸大乱。就算在他最有章法的时刻，他对如何防守也从无半点概念。他只会进攻，别无其他。碰上出拳飞快的攻击者，他就成了废物。肯尼迪的左手三度穿透他的门户。第三记打得他一个趔趄。他还没回过神，肯尼迪的右手已到，沃尔顿扑通瘫倒在地，成了一堆。

他脚板刚沾地就爬起来，可刚爬起来几乎又倒了下去。肯尼迪的拳向来又直又快，如今正当理由与坏脾气合流——第一回合那记犯规的回想，把一副素来平和的心性搅出十足的狠劲——又给他左臂添了一种前所未有的气力，连他自己都感到陌生。他没再用右手。用不着了。

===Original===
Twice more Walton went down. He was still down when Jimmy Silver called time. When the half-minute interval between the rounds was over, he stated that he was not going on.

Kennedy looked across at him as he sat on a bed dabbing tenderly at his face with a handkerchief, and was satisfied with the success of his object-lesson. From his own face the most observant of headmasters could have detected no evidence that he had been engaged in a vulgar fight. Walton, on the other hand, looked as if he had been engaged in several﻿—all violent. Kennedy went off to his study to change, feeling that he had advanced a long step on the thorny path that led to the Perfect House.

===Chinese===
沃尔顿又倒了两回。吉米·西尔弗喊时间到时，他还躺在地上。回合之间的半分钟休整过去之后，他宣布不打了。

肯尼迪望着他坐在床沿，用手帕轻轻擦拭脸庞，对这堂示范课的成效心满意足。至于他自己这张脸，眼神再毒的校长也找不出半点证据，证明他参加过一场庸俗的斗殴。沃尔顿那张脸却不然，看着倒像是打过好几场——场场都激烈。肯尼迪走去书房换衣服，只觉得自己在通往完美宿舍的荆棘路上，迈进了一大步。

## XIV Fenn Receives a Letter / 第十四章 芬恩收到一封信

===Original===
But the step was not such a very long one after all. What it amounted to was simply this, that open rebellion ceased in Kay’s. When Kennedy put up the list on the notice-board for the third time, which he did on the morning following his encounter with Walton, and wrote on it that the match with Blackburn’s would take place that afternoon, his team turned out like lambs, and were duly defeated by thirty-one points. He had to play a substitute for Walton, who was rather too battered to be of any real use in the scrum; but, with that exception, the team that entered the field was the same that should have entered it the day before.

But his labours in the Augean stables of Kay’s were by no means over. Practically they had only begun. The state of the house now was exactly what it had been under Fenn. When Kennedy had taken over the reins, Kay’s had become on the instant twice as bad as it had been before. By his summary treatment of the revolution, he had, so to speak, wiped off this deficit. What he had to do now was to begin to improve things. Kay’s was now in its normal state﻿—slack, rowdy in an underhand way, and utterly useless to the school. It was “up to” Kennedy, as they say in America, to start in and make something presentable and useful out of these unpromising materials.

===Chinese===
不过这一步到底也没有多大。归结起来无非是：凯氏的公开叛乱平息了。同沃尔顿交手的次日早晨，肯尼迪第三次把名单钉上布告栏，写明与布莱克本宿舍的比赛当天下午举行，他的球队便如羔羊般乖乖出洞，如期以三十一分之差吃了败仗。沃尔顿伤得太重，在并列争球里实在顶不了什么用，只好由替补顶他的缺；但除此一人之外，进场的那支队伍，同前一天本该进场的一模一样。

可他在凯氏这座奥吉亚斯牛圈里的苦工远没有完，实际上才刚刚开头。宿舍如今的状态，恰是芬恩治下的老样子。肯尼迪接缰之际，凯氏曾一夜之间坏了一倍；凭着对叛乱快刀斩乱麻的处置，他算是把这笔亏空抹平了。眼下的差事是开始兴利除弊。凯氏如今处于它的常态——懒散、阴着劲儿地吵闹，对全校一无是处。用美国人的话说，现在「该着」肯尼迪出面，从这些不成器的材料里捣鼓出一件像样又有用的东西来。

===Original===
What annoyed him more than anything else was the knowledge that if only Fenn chose to do the square thing and help him in his work, the combination would be irresistible. It was impossible to make any leeway to speak of by himself. If Fenn would only forget his grievances and join forces with him, they could electrify the house.

Fenn, however, showed no inclination to do anything of the kind. He and Kennedy never spoke to one another now except when it was absolutely unavoidable, and then they behaved with that painful politeness in which the public schoolman always wraps himself as in a garment when dealing with a friend with whom he has quarrelled.

On the Walton episode Fenn had made no comment, though it is probable that he thought a good deal.

===Chinese===
最叫他窝火的一点是：只要芬恩肯光明磊落地出手帮他，这对组合将锐不可当。单枪匹马，他连值得一提的进展都难以取得。芬恩只要肯捐弃前嫌、与他合力，两人足叫这宿舍大吃一惊。

然而芬恩丝毫没有此意。如今他和肯尼迪除非万不得已绝不开口，一旦开口，便拿那套叫人牙酸的客客气气彼此相待——公学生同吵翻的朋友打交道，素来要裹上这么一件外衣。

对沃尔顿那段公案，芬恩未置一词，不过他心里多半翻腾了很久。

===Original===
It was while matters were in this strained condition that Fenn received a letter from his elder brother. This brother had been at Eckleton in his time﻿—School House﻿—and had left five years before to go to Cambridge. Cambridge had not taught him a great deal, possibly because he did not meet the well-meant efforts of his tutor halfway. The net result of his three years at King’s was﻿—imprimis, a cricket blue, including a rather lucky eighty-three at Lord’s; secondly, a very poor degree; thirdly and lastly, a taste for literature and the drama﻿—he had been a prominent member of the Footlights Club. When he came down he looked about him for some occupation which should combine in happy proportions a small amount of work and a large amount of salary, and, finding none, drifted into journalism, at which calling he had been doing very fairly ever since.

“Dear Bob,” the letter began. Fenn’s names were Robert Mowbray, the second of which he had spent much of his time in concealing. “Just a line.”

The elder Fenn always began his letters with these words, whether they ran to one sheet or eight. In the present case the screed was not particularly long.

===Chinese===
就在局面如此紧绷之际，芬恩收到他哥哥的一封信。这位兄长当年也在埃克尔顿念过书——学校宿舍的——五年前离校去了剑桥。剑桥没教给他多少东西，可能因为他不肯迎着导师的一片苦心走半步路。他在国王学院三年，净所得如下：其一，一枚板球「蓝」，含在罗德球场颇为侥幸的一次八十三分；其二，一个很不体面的学位；其三，也是最后，对文学与戏剧的趣味——他曾是「脚灯社」的显赫成员。毕业之后他四处物色一份行当，要工作量小小的、薪水大大的，两者比例宜人——没找着，便漂进了新闻界，此后干这一行干得还算像模像样。

「亲爱的鲍勃（Bob）：」信这样开头。芬恩的大名是罗伯特·莫布雷（Robert Mowbray），后一个名字他花了不少工夫去遮掩。「匆匆一笔。」

芬恩哥哥写信向来拿这几个字起头，不论信写满一页还是八页。这一封倒不算特别长。

===Original===
“Do you remember my reading you a bit of an opera I was writing? Well, I finished it, and, after going the round of most of the managers, who chucked it with wonderful unanimity, it found an admirer in Higgs, the man who took the part of the duke in *The Outsider*. Luckily, he happened to be thinking of starting on his own in opera instead of farce, and there’s a part in mine which fits him like a glove. So he’s going to bring it out at the Imperial in the spring, and by way of testing the piece﻿—trying it on the dog, as it were﻿—he means to tour with it. Now, here’s the point of this letter. We start at Eckleton next Wednesday. We shall only be there one night, for we go on to Southampton on Thursday. I suppose you couldn’t come and see it? I remember Peter Brown, who got the last place in the team the year I got my cricket colours, cutting out of his house (Kay’s, by the way) and going down town to see a piece at the theatre. I’m bound to admit he got sacked for it, but still, it shows that it can be done. All the same, I shouldn’t try it on if I were you. You’ll be able to read all about the ‘striking success’ and ‘unrestrained enthusiasm’ in the *Eckleton Mirror* on Thursday. Mind you buy a copy.”

===Chinese===
「还记得我给你念过几段我正写的歌剧吗？喏，写完了。在大多数经理那里转了一圈、被他们以惊人的一致扔出来之后，它遇到了一位赏识者——希格斯（Higgs），就是在《局外人》（*The Outsider*）里演公爵的那位。碰巧他正琢磨着自立门户，改演歌剧不演闹剧，而我这戏里有个角色简直像照着他裁的。所以他打算春天在帝国剧院推出它，并且为试一试这出戏——好比先拿狗试新戏法——他要带它巡演。好，这封信的正题来了：我们下周三在埃克尔顿首演。只待一晚，周四就转赴南安普顿（Southampton）。我想你不能来看一场吧？我记得彼得·布朗（Peter Brown），就是我拿板球队色那年挤进球队末席的那位，翻墙溜出宿舍（说来还是凯氏的）进城看了一场戏。我必须承认他为此被开除了，不过这至少证明：事在人为。话虽如此，我要是你，可不会去冒这个险。『大获成功』『掌声经久不息』云云，你自可在周四的《埃克尔顿镜报》（*Eckleton Mirror*）上读到全文。务必买一份。」

===Original===
The rest of the letter was on other subjects. It took Fenn less than a minute to decide to patronise that opening performance. He was never in the habit of paying very much attention to risks when he wished to do anything, and now he felt as if he cared even less than usual what might be the outcome of the adventure. Since he had ceased to be on speaking terms with Kennedy, he had found life decidedly dull. Kennedy had been his only intimate friend. He had plenty of acquaintances, as a first eleven and first fifteen man usually has, but none of them were very entertaining. Consequently he welcomed the idea of a break in the monotony of affairs. The only thing that had broken it up to the present had been a burglary at the school house. Some enterprising marauder had broken in a week before and gone off with a few articles of value from the headmaster’s drawing-room. But the members of the school house had talked about this episode to such an extent that the rest of the school had dropped off the subject, exhausted, and declined to discuss it further. And things had become monotonous once more.

===Chinese===
信的其余部分谈的是别的。不到一分钟，芬恩就打定主意要光顾那场首演。他想干什么的时候，从不惯于把风险太当回事，此刻他对这场冒险的结局如何，更是觉得比平常还无所谓。自从同肯尼迪绝了交，他发觉生活实在乏味。肯尼迪本是他唯一的至交。他熟人极多——板球一队兼橄榄球一队的人缘向来如此——可没一个有趣的。于是他欢迎任何能打破单调的事。迄今唯一打破过单调的，是学校宿舍的一桩盗窃案。一周前，某个富有开拓精神的小贼破门而入，从校长客厅顺走了几件值钱玩意儿。怎奈学校宿舍的人把这段插曲翻来覆去讲了又讲，讲到全校其余人等精疲力竭、掉头而去，再不肯多议一字。于是万事重回单调。

===Original===
Having decided to go, Fenn began to consider how he should do it. And here circumstances favoured him. It happened that on the evening on which his brother’s play was to be produced the headmaster was giving his once-a-term dinner to the house-prefects. This simplified matters wonderfully. The only time when his absence from the house was at all likely to be discovered would be at prayers, which took place at half-past nine. The prefects’ dinner solved this difficulty for him. Kay would not expect him to be at prayers, thinking he was over at the Head’s, while the Head, if he noticed his absence at all, would imagine that he was staying away from the dinner owing to a headache or some other malady. It seemed tempting Providence not to take advantage of such an excellent piece of luck. For the rest, detection was practically impossible. Kennedy’s advent to the house had ousted Fenn from the dormitory in which he had slept hitherto, and, there being no bed available in any of the other dormitories, he had been put into the spare room usually reserved for invalids whose invalidism was not of a sufficiently infectious kind to demand their removal to the infirmary. As for getting back into the house, he would leave the window of his study unfastened. He could easily climb on to the window-ledge, and so to bed without let or hindrance.

The distance from Kay’s to the town was a mile and a half. If he started at the hour when he should have been starting for the school house, he would arrive just in time to see the curtain go up.

Having settled these facts definitely in his mind, he got his books together and went over to school.

===Chinese===
主意已定，芬恩开始盘算怎么个去法。这一点上，时势偏偏帮他。不巧不成书：他哥哥的戏开演那晚，校长正设那学期一度的级长晚宴。这一下万事大吉。他缺席宿舍一事，唯一可能败露的时机是九点半的晚祷。级长晚宴替他解了这个难题。凯不会指望他到场祷告，只当他人在校长那边；而校长那边呢，纵然留意到他缺席，也会当他是头疼脑热之类的微恙告了假。这样的好运气而不加利用，未免太撩拨天意。至于别的环节，被识破几乎绝无可能。肯尼迪进宿舍，把芬恩从睡惯的寝室里挤了出去，别的寝室又都腾不出床位，他便被打发进了那间空房——那是专留给病号的，病得又不够传染、犯不上挪去医务室的那种。至于回宿舍，他只消不闩书房的窗。翻上窗台易如反掌，从那里上床，一路无人拦阻。

凯氏到镇上一英里半路。他若在原该动身去学校宿舍的那个钟点动身，赶到时恰好见大幕升起。

把这些细节在脑子里一一敲定之后，他收了书，上学去了。

## XV Down Town / 第十五章 进城

===Original===
Fenn arrived at the theatre a quarter of an hour before the curtain rose. Going down a gloomy alley of the High Street, he found himself at the stage door, where he made inquiries of a depressed-looking man with a bad cold in the head as to the whereabouts of his brother. It seemed that he was with Mr. Higgs. If he would wait, said the doorkeeper, his name should be sent up. Fenn waited, while the doorkeeper made polite conversation by describing his symptoms to him in a hoarse growl. Presently the minion who had been despatched to the upper regions with Fenn’s message returned. Would he go upstairs, third door on the left. Fenn followed the instructions, and found himself in a small room, a third of which was filled by a huge iron-bound chest, another third by a very stout man and a dressing-table, while the rest of the space was comparatively empty, being occupied by a wooden chair with three legs. On this seat his brother was trying to balance himself, giving what part of his attention was not required for this feat to listening to some story the fat man was telling him. Fenn had heard his deep voice booming as he went up the passage.

===Chinese===
芬恩在大幕升起前一刻钟到达剧院。顺着高街一条黑黢黢的小巷走下去，他到了后台门口，向一个满面愁容、重感冒缠身的男人打听哥哥的下落。看来哥哥正同希格斯先生在一起。门房说，他若肯稍候，就把他的名姓递上去。芬恩等着，门房则用沙哑的喉音一一描述自己的症状，权作殷勤的寒暄。不多时，被派往楼上送信的小厮回来了：请他上楼，左边第三扇门。芬恩依言而行，进了一间小屋：三分之一被一口铁皮大箱子占据，另外三分之一被一位极胖的先生和一张化妆台瓜分，余下的地面相对空旷，摆着一把三条腿的木椅。他哥哥正努力在那座位上保持平衡，把这项绝技用不完的注意力，拿去听胖子讲的故事。上楼道时芬恩就已听见他那低沉的嗓音嗡嗡作响。

===Original===
His brother did the honours.

“Glad to see you, glad to see you,” said Mr. Higgs, for the fat man was none other than that celebrity. “Take a seat.”

Fenn sat down on the chest and promptly tore his trousers on a jagged piece of iron.

“These provincial dressing rooms!” said Mr. Higgs, by way of comment. “No room! Never any room! No chairs! Nothing!”

He spoke in short, quick sentences, and gasped between each. Fenn said it really didn’t matter﻿—he was quite comfortable.

“Haven’t they done anything about it?” asked Fenn’s brother, resuming the conversation which Fenn’s entrance had interrupted. “We’ve been having a burglary here,” he explained. “Somebody got into the theatre last night through a window. I don’t know what they expected to find.”

===Chinese===
他哥哥替他引见。

「欢迎，欢迎，」希格斯先生说，因为那位胖子不是别人，正是这位名角。「请坐。」

芬恩在铁箱上坐下，立刻被一块翘起的铁皮撕破了裤子。

「外埠的化妆间就是这样！」希格斯先生发表评论，「没地方！从来就没地方！没椅子！什么都没有！」

他说话句子短促，每句之间都要喘一口气。芬恩说真的没关系——他这样挺舒服。

「他们一点动作都没有吗？」芬恩的哥哥接着谈方才被打断的话头。「我们这儿也遭了贼，」他解释道，「昨晚有人从窗户钻进了剧院。我猜不出他们指望找到什么。」

===Original===
“Why,” said Fenn, “we’ve had a burglar up our way too. Chap broke into the school house and went through the old man’s drawing-room. The school house men have been talking about nothing else ever since. I wonder if it’s the same crew.”

Mr. Higgs turned in his chair, and waved a stick of grease paint impressively to emphasise his point.

“There,” he said. “There! What I’ve been saying all along. No doubt of it. Organised gang. And what are the police doing? Nothing, sir, nothing. Making inquiries. Rot! What’s the good of inquiries?”

Fenn’s brother suggested mildly that inquiries were a good beginning. You *must* start somehow. Mr. Higgs scouted the idea.

“There ought not to be any doubt, sir. They ought to *know*. To **know**,” he added, with firmness.

At this point there filtered through the closed doors the strains of the opening chorus.

===Chinese===
「嘿，」芬恩说，「我们那边也来了位贼。一个家伙闯进学校宿舍，把老头子的客厅搜了个遍。学校宿舍的人打那以后别的不谈。不知道是不是同一伙。」

希格斯先生在椅子里转过身，郑重其事地挥动一支油彩，以壮声势。

「瞧，」他说，「瞧！我早就这么说了。毫无疑问。有组织的团伙。可警察在干什么？什么也没干，先生，什么也没干。查问。扯淡！查问顶什么用？」

芬恩的哥哥温和地提议，查问总归是个开头。万事总得有个起头。希格斯先生对此嗤之以鼻。

「就不该有什么疑问，先生。他们就该*知道*。就该**知道**，」他又斩钉截铁地补了一句。

正在此时，开幕合唱的声浪透过关死的门缝渗了进来。

===Original===
“By Jove, it’s begun!” said Fenn’s brother. “Come on, Bob.”

“Where are we going to?” asked Fenn, as he followed. “The wings?”

But it seemed that the rules of Mr. Higgs’ company prevented any outsider taking up his position in that desirable quarter. The only place from which it was possible to watch the performance, except by going to the front of the house, was the “flies,” situated near the roof of the building.

Fenn found all the pleasures of novelty in watching the players from this lofty position. Judged by the cold light of reason, it was not the best place from which to see a play. It was possible to gain only a very foreshortened view of the actors. But it was a change after sitting “in front.”

===Chinese===
「哎呀，开演了！」芬恩的哥哥说，「来吧，鲍勃。」

「咱们去哪儿？」芬恩边跟边问，「侧幕？」

无奈希格斯先生戏班的规矩，不许任何外人占踞那块宝地。要观看演出，除了下楼坐进正厅，唯一的地方就是「天桥」——位于剧场屋顶附近的所在。

从这高处俯瞰演员，芬恩尝到了十足的新鲜乐趣。若拿理性的冷光衡量，这里看戏并非最佳位置，看演员只能得到一种前缩严重的视野。可比起坐在「台下」，总算换个花样。

===Original===
The piece was progressing merrily. The gifted author, at first silent and pale, began now to show signs of gratification. Now and again he chuckled as some jeu de mots hit the mark and drew a quick gust of laughter from the unseen audience. Occasionally he would nudge Fenn to draw his attention to some good bit of dialogue which was approaching. He was obviously enjoying himself.

The advent of Mr. Higgs completed his satisfaction, for the audience greeted the comedian with roars of applause. As a rule Eckleton took its drama through the medium of third-rate touring companies, which came down with plays that had not managed to attract London to any great extent, and were trying to make up for failures in the metropolis by long tours in the provinces. It was seldom that an actor of the Higgs type paid the town a visit, and in a play, too, which had positively never appeared before on any stage. Eckleton appreciated the compliment.

“Listen,” said Fenn’s brother. “Isn’t that just the part for him? It’s just like he was in the dressing room, eh? Short sentences and everything. The funny part of it is that I didn’t know the man when I wrote the play. It was all luck.”

Mr. Higgs’ performance sealed the success of the piece. The house laughed at everything he said. He sang a song in his gasping way, and they laughed still more. Fenn’s brother became incoherent with delight. The verdict of Eckleton was hardly likely to affect London theatregoers, but it was very pleasant notwithstanding. Like every playwright with his first piece, he had been haunted by the idea that his dialogue “would not act,” that, however humorous it might be to a reader, it would fall flat when spoken. There was no doubt now as to whether the lines sounded well.

===Chinese===
戏进展得兴兴头头。那位有才的作者起先一言不发、脸色发白，这时开始露出得色。每当哪句妙语打中靶心、引得看不见的观众迸出一阵短促的笑声，他便咯咯而笑。偶尔他还用胳膊肘捅捅芬恩，提醒他某段好对白快要来了。他显然自得其乐。

希格斯先生的登场使他的快意功德圆满，观众以雷鸣般的喝彩迎接这位喜剧家。埃克尔顿平日的戏剧享受，素来仰仗三流巡回剧团的转手货——带来的戏在伦敦没讨到多大好，便想靠在外省长途巡演弥补京城的失利。希格斯这一级别的演员肯光临小镇，已属罕事，何况带来的还是一出在任何舞台上都从未露过面的新戏。埃克尔顿领受这份抬举。

「听，」芬恩的哥哥说，「这角色给他是不是量身定做？就跟他在化妆间里一个样，对不对？短句子那一套。滑稽的是，我写戏的时候根本不认识这个人。全靠运气。」

希格斯先生的表演给这出戏的成功盖了印。他说什么观众笑什么。他以他那喘式唱法唱了一支歌，观众笑得更凶。芬恩的哥哥欢喜得语无伦次。埃克尔顿的裁决固然左右不了伦敦的看客，可依然叫人受用。同每一位头回交戏的剧作家一样，他一直被一个念头缠着：他的对白「演不出来」——读者读着再妙，一念出口就砸。如今这句台词响不响亮，再无半点疑问。

===Original===
At the beginning of the second act the great Higgs was not on the stage, Fenn’s brother knowing enough of the game not to bring on his big man too soon. He had not to enter for ten minutes or so. The author, who had gone down to see him during the interval, stayed in the dressing room. Fenn, however, who wanted to see all of the piece that he could, went up to the “flies” again.

It occurred to him when he got there that he would see more if he took the seat which his brother had been occupying. It would give him much the same view of the stage, and a wider view of the audience. He thought it would be amusing to see how the audience looked from the “flies.”

Mr. W. S. Gilbert once wrote a poem about a certain bishop who, while fond of amusing himself, objected to his clergy doing likewise. And the consequence was that whenever he did so amuse himself, he was always haunted by a phantom curate, who joined him in his pleasures, much to his dismay. On one occasion he stopped to watch a Punch and Judy show,

“And heard, as Punch was being treated penally,
						
					That phantom curate laughing all hyaenally.”

===Chinese===
第二幕开场时，伟大的希格斯不在台上——芬恩的哥哥深谙此道，不会过早亮出他的大牌。他还有十来分钟才上场。作者幕间休息时下去看他，便留在了化妆室。芬恩却想把这出戏看个尽量全，又上了「天桥」。

到了那儿他忽然想到，若坐进哥哥方才的座位，视野会更开阔。看舞台的角度大体不变，看观众却能多看出好些。他想，从「天桥」上看看观众都是副什么模样，应该挺有趣。

W. S. 吉尔伯特（W. S. Gilbert）先生写过一首诗，讲某位主教：自己贪图行乐，却见不得手下教士如法炮制。结果是，每逢他自己行乐，总有个幻影副牧师阴魂不散地来凑热闹，叫他懊恼不已。有一回他驻足看潘趣与朱迪的木偶戏，

「听见潘趣正被罚得惨，
						
					那幻影副牧师笑得鬣狗一般。」

===Original===
The disgust and panic of this eminent cleric was as nothing compared with that of Fenn, when, shifting to his brother’s seat, he got the first clear view he had had of the audience. In a box to the left of the dress-circle sat, “laughing all hyaenally,” the following distinguished visitors:

- 
						

					Mr. Mulholland of No. 7 College Buildings,

- 
						

					Mr. Raynes of No. 4 ditto,

and

- 
						

					Mr. Kay.

===Chinese===
这位显赫教士的恶心与惊惶，同芬恩换到哥哥座位上、头一回看清观众席时的感受相比，简直不值一提。楼座正厅左侧的包厢里，「笑得鬣狗一般」的，是以下几位贵宾：

- 
						

					学院楼7号的穆赫兰先生（Mr. Mulholland），

- 
						

					同楼4号的雷恩斯先生（Mr. Raynes），

以及

- 
						

					凯先生。

===Original===
Fenn drew back like a flash, knocking his chair over as he did so.

“Giddy, sir?” said a stage hand, pleasantly. “Bless you, lots of gents is like that when they comes up here. Can’t stand the ’eight, they can’t. You’ll be all right in a jiffy.”

“Yes. It﻿—it is rather high, isn’t it?” said Fenn. “Awful glare, too.”

===Chinese===
芬恩闪电般缩了回来，带翻了椅子。

「头晕了吧，先生？」一名舞台工人和气地说，「放心，好多绅士头一回上来都这样。受不了这个『高』，受不了。一会儿就缓过来了。」

「是啊。这——这儿是挺高的，对吧？」芬恩说，「灯光也晃眼得很。」

===Original===
He picked up his chair and sat down well out of sight of the box. Had they seen him? he wondered. Then common sense returned to him. They could not possibly have seen him. Apart from any other reasons, he had only been in his brother’s seat for half-a-dozen seconds. No. He was all right so far. But he would have to get back to the house, and at once. With three of the staff, including his own housemaster, ranging the town, things were a trifle too warm for comfort. He wondered it had not occurred to him that, with a big attraction at the theatre, some of the staff might feel an inclination to visit it.

He did not stop to say goodbye to his brother. Descending from his perch, he hurried to the stage door.

“It’s in the toobs that I feel it, sir,” said the doorkeeper, as he let him out, resuming their conversation as if they had only just parted. Fenn hurried off without waiting to hear more.

===Chinese===
他扶起椅子，挪到包厢绝对看不见的位置坐下。他们瞧见他了吗？他心里嘀咕。随即常识归位：他们不可能瞧见。别的不论，他在哥哥座位上统共只坐了五六秒钟。不，眼下他平安无事。但他必须回宿舍去，立刻。三位教员——包括他自家舍监——正在镇上巡游，这火候未免烫得叫人不安。他纳闷自己怎么就没想到：剧院有大热闹，难免有哪位教员动了来瞧瞧的雅兴。

他没停下来向哥哥道别。下了高座，他直奔后台门。

「俺就是肺管子难受，先生。」门房放他出去时说，接着他们先前的话茬，仿佛两人刚分手片刻。芬恩没等听完就匆匆走了。

===Original===
It was drizzling outside, and there was a fog. Not a “London particular,” but quite thick enough to make it difficult to see where one was going. People and vehicles passed him, vague phantoms in the darkness. Occasionally the former collided with him. He began to wish he had not accepted his brother’s invitation. The unexpected sight of the three masters had shaken his nerve. Till then only the romantic, adventurous side of the expedition had struck him. Now the risks began to loom larger in his mind. It was all very well, he felt, to think, as he had done, that he would be expelled if found out, but that all the same he would risk it. Detection then had seemed a remote contingency. With three masters in the offing it became at least a possibility. The melancholy case of Peter Brown seemed to him now to have a more personal significance for him.

Wrapped in these reflections, he lost his way.

He did not realise this for some time. It was borne in upon him when the road he was taking suddenly came to an abrupt end in a blank wall. Instead of being, as he had fancied, in the High Street, he must have branched off into some miserable blind alley.

More than ever he wished he had not come. Eckleton was not a town that took up a great deal of room on the map of England, but it made up for small dimensions by the eccentricity with which it had been laid out. On a dark and foggy night, to one who knew little of its geography, it was a perfect maze.

===Chinese===
外面下着毛毛雨，还起了雾。倒不是「伦敦特供」那种级别，可也浓得叫人看不清脚下的路。行人和车辆从身边过去，是黑暗中影影绰绰的幽灵。前者偶尔还撞他一下。他开始后悔不该接受哥哥的邀请。三位教员的意外露面动摇了他的胆气。在那之前，此行打动他的只有浪漫冒险的一面。如今风险开始在脑子里放大。他原先那样想——万一被发觉就开除，可开除也认了——本也罢了，彼时被发觉还只是遥远的万一。如今三位教员近在肘腋，它至少成了一种可能。彼得·布朗那桩伤心案例，此刻在他看来，同他本人的干系陡然亲切起来。

他一头扎进这些沉思里，迷了路。

好一阵子他都没意识到。直到脚下的路猛地尽于一堵死墙，他才恍然。他原以为自己走在大街上，想必是半路拐进了哪条可怜的死巷。

他从没像现在这样希望没来过。埃克尔顿在英格兰地图上占地不多，可它以布局之古怪弥补了幅员之小。黑天又起雾，对一个不熟它地理的人而言，它是一座十足的迷宫。

===Original===
Fenn had wandered some way when the sound of someone whistling a popular music-hall song came to him through the gloom. He had never heard anything more agreeable.

“I say,” he shouted at a venture, “can you tell me the way to the High Street?”

The whistler stopped in the middle of a bar, and presently Fenn saw a figure sidling towards him in what struck him as a particularly furtive manner.

“Wot’s thet, gav’nor?”

“Can you tell me where the High Street is? I’ve lost my way.”

The vague figure came closer.

“ ’Igh Street? Yus; yer go﻿—”

===Chinese===
芬恩瞎转了一阵，忽听得黑暗里传来有人用口哨吹一支流行的音乐厅小调。再没有比这更悦耳的声音了。

「我说，」他碰运气地喊了一嗓子，「能告诉我大街怎么走吗？」

吹哨人一个小节吹到一半收了声，不多时芬恩看见一条人影朝自己蹭过来，那副鬼祟劲儿叫他印象深刻。

「啥事儿，老爷？」

「能告诉我大街在哪儿吗？我迷路了。」

模糊的人影凑近了。

「『高』街？中；你走——」

===Original===
A hand shot out, Fenn felt a sharp wrench in the region of his waistcoat, and a moment later the stranger had vanished into the fog with the prefect’s watch and chain.

Fenn forgot his desire to return to the High Street. He forgot everything except that he wished to catch the fugitive, maltreat him, and retrieve his property. He tore in the direction whence came the patter of retreating footsteps.

There were moments when he thought he had him, when he could hear the sound of his breathing. But the fog was against him. Just as he was almost on his man’s heels, the fugitive turned sharply into a street which was moderately well lighted. Fenn turned after him. He had just time to recognise the street as his goal, the High Street, when somebody, walking unexpectedly out of the corner house, stood directly in his path. Fenn could not stop himself. He charged the man squarely, clutched him to save himself, and they fell in a heap on the pavement.

===Chinese===
一只手疾探而出，芬恩只觉马甲一带猛地一拧，转眼间那陌生人已带着级长的怀表和表链消失在雾里。

芬恩忘了自己还要回大街。他什么都忘了，只想着追上逃犯、痛揍一顿、夺回财物。他朝那渐远的脚步啪嗒啪嗒的方向猛追过去。

有几度他觉得要追上了，听得见那人的喘息。可雾帮着对方。眼看他几乎贴上那人的脚跟，逃犯猛地拐进一条灯火尚算明亮的街。芬恩跟着拐过去。他刚认出这条街正是他的目的地——大街——就有人从街角屋子冷不丁走出来，端端正正立在他去路上。芬恩刹不住脚，结结实实撞将上去，一把抱住那人以免跌倒，两人滚作一堆，摔在人行道上。

## XVI What Happened to Fenn / 第十六章 芬恩的遭遇

===Original===
Fenn was up first. Many years’ experience of being tackled at full speed on the football field had taught him how to fall. The stranger, whose football days, if he had ever had any, were long past, had gone down with a crash, and remained on the pavement, motionless. Fenn was conscious of an ignoble impulse to fly without stopping to chat about the matter. Then he was seized with a gruesome fear that he had injured the man seriously, which vanished when the stranger sat up. His first words were hardly of the sort that one would listen to from choice. His first printable expression, which did not escape him until he had been speaking some time, was in the nature of an official bulletin.

“You’ve broken my neck,” said he.

Fenn renewed his apologies and explanations.

“Your watch!” cried the man in a high, cracked voice. “Don’t stand there talking about your watch, but help me up. What do I care about your watch? Why don’t you look where you are going to? Now then, now then, don’t hoist me as if I were a hod of bricks. That’s right. Now help me indoors, and go away.”

===Chinese===
先爬起来的是芬恩。多年在橄榄球场上被人全速擒抱的经验，教会了他怎么摔。那陌生人呢——他若真有过橄榄球的岁月，也早成陈迹——轰然倒地，躺在人行道上一动不动。芬恩心头掠过一股不甚光彩的冲动：拔腿就跑，不留下讨论此事。随即他又惊惧起来，生怕把那人摔出了好歹，直到陌生人坐起身，这层恐惧才散了。那人的头几句话，实在不是人家甘愿洗耳恭听的那一类。他讲了好一会儿才漏出的第一句可入印刷品的表态，性质上属于官方公报。

「你把我的脖子摔断了。」他说。

芬恩又赔罪又解释。

「你的怀表！」那人扯着又高又破的嗓门嚷道，「别站在那儿念叨你的怀表了，扶我起来。你的怀表关我什么事？你自己走路怎么不看着点儿？哎，哎，别像吊一垛砖似的拽我。这就对了。现在扶我进屋，然后滚蛋。」

===Original===
Fenn supported him while he walked lamely into the house. He was relieved to find that there was nothing more the matter with him than a shaking and a few bruises.

“Door on the left,” said the injured one.

Fenn led him down the passage and into a small sitting-room. The gas was lit, and as he turned it up he saw that the stranger was a man well advanced in years. He had grey hair that was almost white. His face was not a pleasant one. It was a mass of lines and wrinkles from which a physiognomist would have deduced uncomplimentary conclusions as to his character. Fenn had little skill in that way, but he felt that for some reason he disliked the man, whose eyes, which were small and extraordinarily bright, gave rather an eerie look to his face.

“Go away, go away,” he kept repeating savagely from his post on the shabby sofa on which Fenn had deposited him.

===Chinese===
芬恩搀着他，一瘸一拐走进屋去。见他不过是震了一下、添了几块淤伤，并无大碍，芬恩松了口气。

「左边的门。」伤号说。

芬恩领他沿过道进了一间小客厅。煤气灯点着，他捻亮灯芯，看清这陌生人已入暮年，头发灰得近乎全白。这张脸不讨人喜欢：沟壑纵横，皱纹累累，相面先生见了准会就此人的品性推断出些不客气的结论。芬恩于相面一道并无造诣，却不知怎的就是不喜欢此人。那双眼睛小得出奇又亮得出奇，给这张脸平添几分阴森。

「走开，走开。」他窝在芬恩安顿他的那张破沙发上，恶声恶气地念叨个没完。

===Original===
“But are you all right? Can’t I get you something?” asked the Eckletonian.

“Go away, go away,” repeated the man.

Conversation on these lines could never be really attractive. Fenn turned to go. As he closed the door and began to feel his way along the dark passage, he heard the key turn in the lock behind him. The man could not, he felt, have been very badly hurt if he were able to get across the room so quickly. The thought relieved him somewhat. Nobody likes to have the maiming even of the most complete stranger on his mind. The sensation of relief lasted possibly three seconds. Then it flashed upon him that in the excitement of the late interview he had forgotten his cap. That damaging piece of evidence lay on the table in the sitting-room, and between him and it was a locked door.

He groped his way back, and knocked. No sound came from the room.

“I say,” he cried, “you might let me have my cap. I left it on the table.”

No reply.

===Chinese===
「可您没事吧？要不要我给您拿点什么？」这位埃克尔顿人问道。

「走开，走开。」那人重复道。

照这个路数谈下去，实在谈不上引人入胜。芬恩转身要走。他掩上门，正要摸索着走过黑过道，忽听身后钥匙在锁孔里一转。他想，那人能这么快横穿房间，伤得必不重。这念头让他宽慰了些。哪怕残害的是素不相识的陌路人，也没人乐意搁在心上。这份宽慰大约持续了三秒钟。随即他猛然想起，方才会面的紧张忙乱之中，他忘了帽子。那件致罪的物证就躺在客厅桌上，而横在他与它之间的，是一扇反锁的门。

他摸回去敲门。屋里悄无声息。

「我说，」他喊道，「行行好把帽子还我。我落在桌上了。」

没有回音。

===Original===
Fenn half thought of making a violent assault on the door. He refrained on reflecting that it would be useless. If he could break it open﻿—which, in all probability, he could not﻿—there would be trouble such as he had never come across in his life. He was not sure it would not be an offence for which he would be rendered liable to fine or imprisonment. At any rate, it would mean the certain detection of his visit to the town. So he gave the thing up, resolving to return on the morrow and reopen negotiations. For the present, what he had to do was to get safely back to his house. He had lost his watch, his cap with his name in it was in the hands of an evil old man who evidently bore him a grudge, and he had to run the gauntlet of three housemasters and get to bed via a study-window. Few people, even after the dullest of plays, have returned from the theatre so disgusted with everything as did Fenn. Reviewing the situation as he ran with long, easy strides over the road that led to Kay’s, he found it devoid of any kind of comfort.

===Chinese===
芬恩一度想对那扇门施以暴力。转念一想毫无用处，作罢。就算他能破门——多半不能——接下来的麻烦，将是他平生未见的。他吃不准这算不算一桩罪，够得上罚款或蹲监。退一万步说，这至少意味着他进城之事铁定败露。于是他死了这条心，决计明日再来重开谈判。眼下当务之急，是平平安安回到宿舍。怀表丢了，写着名字的帽子攥在一个显然记恨于他的歹毒老头手里，还得闯三位舍监的刀山，最后从书房窗户爬上床。即便看完最乏味的一出戏，也很少有人像芬恩这样，从剧院回来对万事如此倒尽胃口。他迈开轻松的大步，在通往凯氏的路上边跑边检讨局势，发觉其中找不出半点安慰。

===Original===
It is just as well that the gift of second sight is accorded to but few. If Fenn could have known at this point that his adventures were only beginning, that what had taken place already was but as the overture to a drama, it is possible that he would have thrown up the sponge for good and all, entered Kay’s by way of the front door﻿—after knocking up the entire household﻿—and remarked, in answer to his housemaster’s excited questions, “Enough! Enough! I am a victim of Fate, a Toad beneath the Harrow. Sack me tomorrow, if you like, but for goodness’ sake let me get quietly to bed now.”

As it was, not being able to “peep with security into futurity,” he imagined that the worst was over.

He began to revise this opinion immediately on turning in at Kay’s gate. He had hardly got halfway down the drive when the front door opened and two indistinct figures came down the steps. As they did so his foot slipped off the grass border on which he was running to deaden the noise of his steps, and grated sharply on the gravel.

===Chinese===
先见之明这种天赋，只赋予少数人，倒也是件好事。倘若芬恩此刻得知，他的历险才刚刚开场，先前种种不过是一出戏的序曲，他很可能就此永远缴械，走前门进凯氏——把全家上下都敲起来——并且在回答舍监气急败坏的盘问时说：「够了！够了！我是命运的牺牲品，是耙子底下的癞蛤蟆。要开除明天开除，看在老天的份上，先让我安安静静上床。」

实际情况是，他既不能「安然窥探未来」，便以为最糟的已经过去。

一拐进凯氏大门，他立刻开始修正这一看法。他沿车道才走到一半，前门开了，两条模糊的人影走下台阶。就在这时，他原本踩着借以消音的草皮边沿一滑，脚底下在砾石上擦出刺耳的一声。

===Original===
“What’s that?” said a voice. The speaker was Mr. Kay.

“What’s what?” replied a second voice which he recognised as Mr. Mulholland’s.

“Didn’t you hear a noise?”

“‘I heard the water lapping on the crag,’” replied Mr. Mulholland, poetically.

“It was over there,” persisted Mr. Kay. “I am certain I heard something﻿—positively certain, Mulholland. And after that burglary at the school house﻿—”

He began to move towards the spot where Fenn lay crouching behind a bush. Mr. Mulholland followed, mildly amused. They were a dozen yards away when Fenn, debating in his mind whether it would not be better﻿—as it would certainly be more dignified﻿—for him to rise and deliver himself up to justice instead of waiting to be discovered wallowing in the damp grass behind a laurel bush, was aware of something soft and furry pressing against his knuckles. A soft purring sound reached his ears.

===Chinese===
「那是什么？」一个声音说。说话的是凯先生。

「什么是什么？」第二个声音答道，他听出是穆赫兰先生的。

「你没听见响动？」

「『我听见海水拍打着礁石，』」穆赫兰先生诗意盎然地答道。

「就在那边，」凯先生执意道，「我肯定听见了什么——绝对肯定，穆赫兰。何况学校宿舍刚遭过贼——」

他朝芬恩趴着的那丛灌木后头挪过去。穆赫兰先生跟着，觉得颇有几分好玩。两人还差十几码时，芬恩正心里掂量：与其等着被发现趴在月桂丛后湿漉漉的草里，是不是不如自己站起来投案自首——那样肯定更体面些——忽觉指节上挨了一团毛茸茸软乎乎的东西。一阵轻柔的呼噜声传入耳中。

===Original===
He knew at once who it was﻿—Thomas Edward, the matron’s cat, ever a staunch friend of his. Many a time had they taken tea together in his study in happier days. The friendly animal had sought him out in his hiding-place, and was evidently trying to intimate that the best thing they could do now would be to make a regular night of it.

Fenn, as I have said, liked and respected Thomas. In ordinary circumstances he would not have spoken an unfriendly word to him. But things were desperate now, and needed remedies to match.

Very softly he passed his hand down the delighted animal’s back until he reached his tail. Then, stifling with an effort all the finer feelings which should have made such an act impossible, he administered so vigorous a tweak to that appendage that Thomas, with one frenzied yowl, sprang through the bush past the two masters and vanished at full speed into the opposite hedge.

“My goodness!” said Mr. Kay, starting back.

It was a further shock to Fenn to find how close he was to the laurel.

===Chinese===
他立刻知道这是谁——托马斯·爱德华（Thomas Edward），女管家的猫，素来是他的忠贞朋友。想当年好时光里，他俩不知在他书房里共进过多少回茶点。这只友好的动物寻到了他的藏身之处，显然是想表示：如今上策，莫过于痛痛快快玩它一整夜。

如前所说，芬恩对托马斯又喜欢又敬重。寻常时势下，他绝无对它口出恶言的道理。可眼下事情紧急，须用猛药。

他把手轻轻顺着这只心满意足的动物的脊背抚下去，一直摸到尾巴。随后，他奋力扼杀了一切本该令此举无从下手的美好情操，朝那截附属物狠狠一拧。托马斯发出一声凄厉的惨嗥，穿丛而出，掠过两位先生，全速消失在对面的树篱里。

「我的天！」凯先生惊得倒退。

芬恩也再受一重惊吓：他们离月桂丛竟这么近了。

===Original===
“‘Goodness me,
						
					Why, what was that?
						
					Silent be,
						
					It was the cat,’”

chanted Mr. Mulholland, who was in poetical vein after the theatre.

“It was a cat!” gasped Mr. Kay.

“So I am disposed to imagine. What lungs! We shall be having the R.S.P.C.A. down on us if we aren’t careful. They must have heard that noise at the headquarters of the Society, wherever they are. Well, if your zeal for big game hunting is satisfied, and you don’t propose to follow the vocalist through that hedge, I think I will be off. Good night. Good piece, wasn’t it?”

“Excellent. Good night, Mulholland.”

===Chinese===
「『哎呀妈，
						
					那是什么呀？
						
					别作声吧，
						
					原来是猫哇，』」

穆赫兰先生吟诵道——看罢戏，他正诗兴大发。

「是只猫！」凯先生喘着气说。

「我也正这么想。好一副肺活量！咱们再不当心，皇家防止虐待动物协会（R.S.P.C.A.）就要找上门了。协会总部，不管设在哪儿，这动静他们必定听见了。好了，您这番猎猛兽的热忱既已满足，又不打算追那位歌唱家钻树篱，我就告辞了。晚安。戏不错吧？」

「好戏。晚安，穆赫兰。」

===Original===
“By the way, I wonder if the man who wrote it is a relation of our Fenn. It may be his brother﻿—I believe he writes. You probably remember him when he was here. He was before my time. Talking of Fenn, how do you find the new arrangement answer? Is Kennedy an improvement?”

“Kennedy,” said Mr. Kay, “is a well-meaning boy, I think. Quite well-meaning. But he lacks ability, in my opinion. I have had to speak to him on several occasions on account of disturbances amongst the juniors. Once I found two boys actually fighting in the junior dayroom. I was very much annoyed about it.”

“And where was Kennedy while this was going on? Was he holding the watch?”

“The watch?” said Mr. Kay, in a puzzled tone of voice. “Kennedy was over at the gymnasium when it occurred.”

“Then it was hardly his fault that the fight took place.”

“My dear Mulholland, if the head of a house is efficient, fights should be impossible. Even when he is not present, his influence, his prestige, so to speak, should be sufficient to restrain the boys under him.”

===Chinese===
「对了，不知写这戏的人是不是咱们芬恩的亲戚。没准是他哥哥——我记得他是动笔杆子的。他在这儿的时候您多半还记得。那就比我早了。说起芬恩，您觉得新安排效果如何？肯尼迪算改善吗？」

「肯尼迪嘛，」凯先生说，「我认为是个好心的孩子。相当好心。但依我看缺乏才干。低年级生闹事，我好几次不得不训诫他。有一回我亲眼撞见两个男孩在低年级活动室里打架。我为此大为光火。」

「这事进行的时候肯尼迪在哪儿呢？在掌表吗？」

「掌表？」凯先生一脸茫然，「事情发生时，肯尼迪在体育馆那边。」

「那这场架就怪不着他了吧。」

「亲爱的穆赫兰，舍长若真得力，打架根本就该无从发生。哪怕他不在场，他的影响、他的威望，可以说，也足以约束住他手下的男孩。」

===Original===
Mr. Mulholland whistled softly.

“So that’s your idea of what the head of your house should be like, is it? Well, I know of one fellow who would have been just your man. Unfortunately, he is never likely to come to school at Eckleton.”

“Indeed?” said Mr. Kay, with interest. “Who is that? Where did you meet him? What school is he at?”

“I never said I had met him. I only go by what I have heard of him. And as far as I know, he is not at any school. He was a gentleman of the name of Napoleon Bonaparte. He might just have been equal to the arduous duties which devolve upon the head of your house. Good night.”

And Fenn heard his footsteps crunch the gravel as he walked away. A minute later the front door shut, and there was a rattle. Mr. Kay had put the chain up and retired for the night.

===Chinese===
穆赫兰先生轻轻吹了声口哨。

「原来这就是您心目中舍长该有的样子，是吗？好啊，我知道有一位正合您的意。可惜，他绝无可能来埃克尔顿上学。」

「哦？」凯先生来了兴趣，「是谁？您在哪儿见过他？他在什么学校？」

「我从来没说见过他。我不过是凭对他的传闻说话。据我所知，他不在任何学校。他是位名叫拿破仑·波拿巴（Napoleon Bonaparte）的先生。您府上舍长的那些艰巨职责，他兴许勉强胜任。晚安。」

芬恩听着他的脚步碾过砾石远去。一分钟后前门关上，哗啦一响。凯先生挂上门链，安寝去了。

===Original===
Fenn lay where he was for a short while longer. Then he rose, feeling very stiff and wet, and crept into one of the summerhouses which stood in Mr. Kay’s garden. Here he sat for an hour and a half, at the end of which time, thinking that Mr. Kay must be asleep, he started out to climb into the house.

His study was on the first floor. A high garden-seat stood directly beneath the window and acted as a convenient ladder. It was easy to get from this on to the window-ledge. Once there he could open the window, and the rest would be plain sailing.

Unhappily, there was one flaw in his scheme. He had conceived that scheme in the expectation that the window would be as he had left it.

But it was not.

During his absence somebody had shot the bolt. And, try his hardest, he could not move the sash an inch.

===Chinese===
芬恩在原地又趴了一小会儿，然后起身，浑身僵硬透湿，溜进凯先生花园里的一座凉亭。他在里面坐了一个半钟头，估摸凯先生必已入睡，才动身往屋里爬。

他的书房在二楼。窗下正对着一座高脚花园座椅，恰是一架现成的梯子。从座椅上窗台易如反掌。上了窗台就能开窗，往后便一帆风顺。

不幸，这计划有一处破绽。他构思时，指望那扇窗保持他离开时的原样。

可它没有。

他不在的当口，有人插上了插销。任他使尽浑身解数，窗扇一寸也推不动。

## XVII Fenn Hunts for Himself / 第十七章 芬恩捉拿自己

===Original===
Nobody knows for certain the feelings of the camel when his proprietor placed that last straw on his back. The incident happened so long ago. If it had occurred in modern times, he would probably have contributed a firsthand report to the *Daily Mail*. But it is very likely that he felt on that occasion exactly as Fenn felt when, after a night of unparalleled misadventure, he found that somebody had cut off his retreat by latching the window. After a gruelling race Fate had just beaten him on the tape.

There was no doubt about its being latched. The sash had not merely stuck. He put all he knew into the effort to raise it, but without a hint of success. After three attempts he climbed down again and, sitting on the garden-seat, began to review his position.

If one has an active mind and a fair degree of optimism, the effect of the “staggerers” administered by Fate passes off after a while. Fenn had both. The consequence was that, after ten minutes of grey despair, he was relieved by a faint hope that there might be some other way into the house than through his study. Anyhow, it would be worth while to investigate.

===Chinese===
谁也说不准，当年骆驼的主人在它背上压上最后一根稻草时，它心里是什么滋味。那件事年头太久远了。若是发生在现代，它多半会给《每日邮报》（*Daily Mail*）投一篇第一手报道。不过十有八九，它当时的感受，同此刻的芬恩（Fenn）一模一样——经过一夜无与伦比的倒霉历险之后，他发现有人扣上了窗户，切断了他的退路。一场筋疲力尽的赛跑下来，命运恰好在终点线上险胜了他。

窗户扣上了，这一点毫无疑问。窗框并不是卡住了那么简单。他使出浑身解数想抬起窗子，却连一丝成功的迹象都没有。试了三次之后，他重新爬下来，坐到花园长椅上，开始盘点自己的处境。

一个人只要脑子活络，又有几分乐观，命运那记「当头闷棍」的效力过上一阵便会消退。芬恩两样兼备。于是，在十分钟的灰心绝望之后，一线微弱的希望让他缓了过来：除了从书房进屋，说不定还有别的路。无论如何，值得一探。

===Original===
His study was at the side of the house. At the back were the kitchen, the scullery, and the dining-room, and above these more studies and a couple of dormitories. As a last resort he might fling rocks and other solids at the windows until he woke somebody up. But he did not feel like trying this plan until every other had failed. He had no desire to let a garrulous dormitory into the secret of his wanderings. What he hoped was that he might find one of the lower windows open.

And so he did.

As he turned the corner of the house he saw what he had been looking for. The very first window was wide open. His spirits shot up, and for the first time since he had left the theatre he was conscious of taking a pleasure in his adventurous career. Fate was with him after all. He could not help smiling as he remembered how he had felt during that ten minutes on the garden-seat, when the future seemed blank and devoid of any comfort whatsoever. And all the time he could have got in without an effort, if he had only thought of walking half a dozen yards.

===Chinese===
他的书房在房子侧面。房子后面是厨房、洗涤室和餐厅，餐厅楼上则是另几间书房和两间寝室。实在走投无路，他还可以朝窗户扔石块之类的硬物，直到把什么人砸醒。但在其他一切办法都宣告失败之前，他可不想试这一招。他不愿意让一间嘴碎的寝室得知他夜游的秘密。他指望的是，也许能碰上哪扇楼下的窗户开着。

还真让他碰上了。

绕过房子的墙角，他便看到了要找的东西。头一扇窗户就敞得大开。他精神一振——自从离开戏院以来，他头一回觉出这场冒险生涯里有点乐趣。命运到底还是站在他这边的。想到自己在花园长椅上那十分钟里的心境——前途一片漆黑，没有半点安慰——他忍不住笑了。其实他本来毫不费力就能进来，只要想到多走上几码路。

===Original===
Now that the way was open to him, he wasted no time. He climbed through into the dark room. He was not certain which room it was, in spite of his lengthy residence at Kay’s.

He let himself down softly till his foot touched the floor. After a moment’s pause he moved forward a step. Then another. At the third step his knee struck the leg of a table. He must be in the dining-room. If so, he was all right. He could find his way up to his room with his eyes shut. It was easy to find out for certain. The walls of the dining-room at Kay’s, as in the other houses, were covered with photographs. He walked gingerly in the direction in which he imagined the nearest wall to be, reached it, and passed his hand along it. Yes, there were photographs. Then all he had to do was to find the table again, make his way along it, and when he got to the end the door would be a yard or so to his left. The programme seemed simple and attractive. But it was added to in a manner which he had not foreseen. Feeling his way back to the table, he upset a chair. If he had upset a cartload of coal on to a sheet of tin it could not, so it seemed to him in the disordered state of his nerves, have made more noise. It went down with an appalling crash, striking the table on its way. “This,” thought Fenn, savagely, as he waited, listening, “is where I get collared. What a fool I am to barge about like this.”

He felt that the echoes of that crash must have penetrated to every corner of the house. But no one came. Perhaps, after all, the noise had not been so great. He proceeded on his journey down the table, feeling every inch of the way. The place seemed one bristling mass of chairs. But, by the exercise of consummate caution, he upset no more and won through at last in safety to the door.

===Chinese===
路既然通了，他也就不耽搁。他翻进黑黢黢的房间。虽说在凯氏宿舍（Kay’s）住了这么久，他还真说不准这是哪间屋。

他轻轻落下，直到脚尖触到地板。顿了一顿，他往前挪了一步，又一步。第三步上，膝盖撞上了桌子腿。那他准是在餐厅里。若是这样就好办了，闭上眼睛他也能摸回自己房间。要确证这一点很容易。凯氏宿舍的餐厅同别的宿舍一样，墙上挂满了照片。他小心翼翼地朝自己估摸的最近一面墙摸去，摸到了墙，便伸手沿墙抚过去。没错，是照片。那么接下来只要再找到那张桌子，沿着桌子摸过去，到头之后门就在左手边一码开外。这个方案看上去简单又稳妥。不料却节外生枝，出了他始料未及的一枝。往回摸桌子的时候，他撞翻了一把椅子。以他当时心神不宁的状态来看，就算把一整车煤倒在铁皮上，响声也不过如此。椅子轰然倒地，半路还磕上了桌子。「这一下，」芬恩竖着耳朵等着，恶狠狠地想，「该被人当场拿住了。我这样瞎撞乱闯，多蠢啊。」

他觉得那一声巨响的回音必定已经钻进了屋子每个角落。可是没有人来。也许响声终究没有想象中大。他继续沿着桌子往下摸，一寸一寸地探着走。这地方椅子林立，仿佛浑身是刺。但凭着登峰造极的小心，他再没撞翻一把，终于安然摸到了门口。

===Original===
It was at this point that the really lively and exciting part of his adventure began. Compared with what was to follow, his evening had been up to the present dull and monotonous.

As he opened the door there was a sudden stir and crash at the other end of the room. Fenn had upset one chair and the noise had nearly deafened him. Now chairs seemed to be falling in dozens. Bang! Bang! Crash!! (two that time). And then somebody shot through the window like a harlequin and dashed away across the lawn. Fenn could hear his footsteps thudding on the soft turf. And at the same moment other footsteps made themselves heard.

Somebody was coming downstairs.

“Who is that? Is anybody there?”

===Chinese===
恰恰在此时，他那场冒险里真正有声有色、惊险刺激的部分才开始。同接下来发生的事相比，他这一晚到目前为止都算得上沉闷单调。

他一开门，房间另一头猛地响起一阵骚动与撞击。芬恩方才只撞翻一把椅子，那响声已震得他半聋；如今椅子仿佛成打成打地往下倒。砰！砰！轰隆！！（这回是两把。）紧接着，有人像哑剧里的丑角似的从窗口一蹿而出，飞奔着穿过草坪。芬恩听得见他踏在松软草皮上的噗噗脚步声。与此同时，另一些脚步声也响了起来。

有人下楼来了。

「谁在那儿？屋里有人吗？」

===Original===
It was Mr. Kay’s voice, unmistakably nervous. Fenn darted from the door and across the passage. At the other side was a boot-cupboard. It was his only refuge in that direction. What he ought to have done was to leave the dining-room by the opposite door, which led via a corridor to the junior dayroom. But he lost his head, and instead of bolting away from the enemy, went towards him.

The stairs down which Mr. Kay was approaching were at the end of the passage. To reach the dining-room one turned to the right. Beyond the stairs on the left the passage ended in a wall, so that Mr. Kay was bound to take the right direction in the search. Fenn wondered if he had a pistol. Not that he cared very much. If the housemaster was going to find him, it would be very little extra discomfort to be shot at. And Mr. Kay’s talents as a marksman were in all probability limited to picking off sitting haystacks. The important point was that he had a candle. A faint yellow glow preceded him down the stairs. Playing hide-and-seek with him in the dark, Fenn might have slipped past in safety; but the candle made that impossible.

===Chinese===
那是凯先生（Mr. Kay）的声音，明显透着紧张。芬恩从门口窜出，横穿走廊。走廊另一侧有一间靴柜间，那是他这个方向上唯一的藏身处。他本该从餐厅另一头的门出去才对——那扇门经一条过道通向低年级活动室——可他慌了神，不是避开敌人逃，反倒朝敌人迎了上去。

凯先生正顺着楼梯下来，楼梯在走廊尽头。去餐厅得往右拐。楼梯再往左，走廊尽头是堵墙，所以凯先生搜寻时必定得往右走。芬恩琢磨他有没有手枪。倒也不是很在乎。舍监要真找到他，再挨一枪也添不了多少苦头。何况凯先生的枪法，充其量也就是打打一动不动的干草堆。要紧的是他手里有支蜡烛。一团昏黄的光晕先他一步下了楼梯。若是在黑暗里同他捉迷藏，芬恩本可以安然从他身边溜过去；可那支蜡烛让这成了泡影。

===Original===
He found the boot-room door and slipped through just as Mr. Kay turned the corner. With a thrill of pleasure he found that there was a key inside. He turned it as quietly as he could, but nevertheless it grated. Having done this, and seeing nothing else that he could do except await developments, he sat down on the floor among the boots. It was not a dignified position for a man who had played for his county while still at school, but just then he would not have exchanged it for a throne﻿—if the throne had been placed in the passage or the dining-room.

The only question was﻿—had he been seen or heard? He thought not; but his heart began to beat furiously as the footsteps stopped outside the cupboard door and unseen fingers rattled the handle.

Twice Mr. Kay tried the handle, but, finding the cupboard locked, passed on into the dining-room. The light of the candle ceased to shine under the door, and Fenn was once more in inky darkness.

===Chinese===
他摸到靴柜间的门，恰在凯先生转过墙角的一瞬闪身而入。他一阵窃喜：门里插着钥匙。他尽量轻地拧动它，还是发出了一声涩响。做完这件事，眼看再无别的事可做，只有静候事态发展，他便在靴子堆里席地坐下了。对一个还在校读书就代表郡队打过球的人来说，这姿势实在算不上体面；可当时只要那宝座不是摆在走廊或餐厅里，拿宝座来换他都不干。

唯一的问题是——他有没有被看见、被听见？他想是没有；可是当脚步声停在柜门外、看不见的手指拨响门把手时，他的心还是狂跳起来。

凯先生转了两下把手，发现柜门锁着，便径直往餐厅去了。烛光从门缝下消失了，芬恩重新陷入墨一般的黑暗。

===Original===
He listened intently. A minute later he had made his second mistake. Instead of waiting, as he should have done, until Mr. Kay had retired for good, he unlocked the door directly he had passed, and when a muffled crash told him that the housemaster was in the dining-room among the chairs, out he came and fled softly upstairs towards his bedroom. He thought that Mr. Kay might possibly take it into his head to go round the dormitories to make certain that all the members of his house were in. In which case all would be discovered.

When he reached his room he began to fling off his clothes with feverish haste. Once in bed all would be well.

===Chinese===
他凝神细听。一分钟后，他犯了第二个错误。他本该等凯先生彻底回房歇下再动，却在那人刚一走过时就开了锁；等到一声闷响告诉他舍监已经进了餐厅、正在椅子堆里时，他溜出门，蹑手蹑脚地逃上楼回寝室去了。他心想，凯先生说不定会起意到各寝室转上一圈，确认全宿舍的人都到齐了。真那样的话，一切就都露馅了。

一回到房间，他便火烧火燎地脱起衣服来。只要钻进被窝，就万事大吉。

===Original===
He had got out of his boots, his coat, and his waistcoat, and was beginning to feel that electric sensation of triumph which only comes to the man who *just* pulls through, when he heard Mr. Kay coming down the corridor towards his room. The burglar-hunter, returning from the dining-room in the full belief that the miscreant had escaped through the open window, had had all his ardour for the chase redoubled by the sight of the cupboard door, which Fenn in his hurry had not remembered to close. Mr. Kay had made certain by two separate trials that that door had been locked. And now it was wide open. Ergo, the apostle of the jemmy and the skeleton key must still be in the house. Mr. Kay, secure in the recollection that burglars never show fight if they can possibly help it, determined to search the house.

Fenn made up his mind swiftly. There was no time to finish dressing. Mr. Kay, peering round, might note the absence of the rest of his clothes from their accustomed pegs if he got into bed as he was. There was only one thing to be done. He threw back the bedclothes, ruffled the sheets till the bed looked as if it had been slept in, and opened the door just as Mr. Kay reached the threshold.

===Chinese===
靴子脱了，外套脱了，马甲也脱了，他正开始体味那种只有*堪堪*脱险的人才尝得到的、电击般的胜利感，忽听得凯先生沿走廊朝他房间走来。这位缉贼猎手从餐厅折返，满心以为歹徒已从那扇敞开的窗户逃之夭夭；不料一看见那扇靴柜间的门——芬恩慌乱中忘了关——追敌的热忱顿时翻了一倍。凯先生此前分明试过两回，确证那扇门锁着。如今它却敞得大开。由此可见，那位惯使撬棍与万能钥匙的行家必定还在屋里。凯先生深信窃贼但凡有办法绝不动武，便打定主意把全屋搜个遍。

芬恩当机立断。来不及把衣服脱完了。要是就这么上了床，凯先生四下一扫，兴许会察觉衣钩上少了他其余的衣裳。办法只有一个。他掀开被褥，把床单揉得皱皱巴巴，让床看上去像睡过人，然后拉开门——恰好赶在凯先生迈到门槛前。

===Original===
“Anything the matter, sir?” asked Fenn, promptly. “I heard a noise downstairs. Can I help you?”

Mr. Kay looked carefully at the ex-head of his house. Fenn was a finely-developed youth. He stood six feet, and all of him that was not bone was muscle. A useful colleague to have by one in a hunt for a possibly ferocious burglar.

So thought Mr. Kay.

“So *you* heard the noise?” he said. “Well, perhaps you had better come with me. There is no doubt that a burglar has entered the house tonight, in spite of the fact that I locked all the windows myself. Your study window was unlocked, Fenn. It was extremely careless of you to leave it in such a condition, and I hope you will be more careful in future. Why, somebody might have got in through it.”

Fenn thought it was not at all unlikely.

“Come along, then. I am sure the man is still in the house. He was hiding in the cupboard by the dining-room. I know it. I am sure he is still in the house.”

But, in spite of the fact that Fenn was equally sure, half an hour’s search failed to discover any lurking evildoer.

===Chinese===
「出什么事了吗，先生？」芬恩抢先把话堵上，「我听见楼下一声响。要我来帮忙吗？」

凯先生仔细打量这位前任舍长。芬恩是个发育极佳的青年，身高六英尺，除了骨头全是肌肉。追捕一名说不定穷凶极恶的窃贼时，有这么一位同行，可是个得力的帮手。

凯先生正是这么想的。

「原来是*你*听见了响动？」他说，「好吧，那你最好跟我来。毫无疑问，今晚有窃贼进了屋，尽管所有窗户都是我亲手锁好的。你那间书房的窗户就没锁，芬恩。把窗户留在那种状态，实在太大意了，望你往后多加小心。弄不好就会有人从那儿钻进来呢。」

芬恩心想，这倒一点也不假。

「那就走吧。我敢肯定那人还在屋里。他方才就藏在餐厅旁边的柜子里。我知道的。我确信他还在屋里。」

然而，尽管芬恩同样确信，半个小时的搜查也没能揪出任何潜伏的歹徒。

===Original===
“You had better go to bed, Fenn,” said Mr. Kay, disgustedly, at the end of that period. “He must have got back in some extraordinary manner.”

“Yes, sir,” agreed Fenn.

He himself had certainly got back in a very extraordinary manner.

However, he *had* got back, which was the main point.

===Chinese===
「你最好去睡吧，芬恩。」半小时下来，凯先生懊丧地说，「他八成是用什么邪门的法子跑掉的。」

「是，先生。」芬恩附和道。

他自己进来的法子，倒确实是够邪门的。

不过他到底是进来了，这才是主要的。

## XVIII A Vain Quest / 第十八章 徒劳的寻找

===Original===
After all he had gone through that night, it disturbed Fenn very little to find on the following morning that the professional cracksman had gone off with one of the cups in his study. Certainly, it was not as bad as it might have been, for he had only abstracted one out of the half dozen that decorated the room. Fenn was a fine runner, and had won the “sprint” events at the sports for two years now.

The news of the burglary at Kay’s soon spread about the school. Mr. Kay mentioned it to Mr. Mulholland, and Mr. Mulholland discussed it at lunch with the prefects of his house. The juniors of Kay’s were among the last to hear of it, but when they did, they made the most of it, to the disgust of the School House fags, to whom the episode seemed in the nature of an infringement of copyright. Several spirited by-battles took place that day owing to this, and at the lower end of the table of Kay’s dining-room at tea that evening there could be seen many swollen countenances. All, however, wore pleased smiles. They had proved to the School House their right to have a burglary of their own if they liked. It was the first occasion since Kennedy had become head of the house that Kay’s had united in a common and patriotic cause.

===Chinese===
经历了那一夜的种种，第二天早晨发现那位「职业惯偷」顺手带走了他书房里的一只奖杯，芬恩几乎不为所动。好在这事不算太糟——屋里陈列着半打奖杯，对方只抄走了一只。芬恩是跑得飞快的人，校运动会上的「短跑」项目，他已经连赢两年了。

凯氏宿舍失窃的消息很快传遍全校。凯先生向马尔霍兰先生（Mr. Mulholland）提起，马尔霍兰先生又在午饭时同他宿舍的级长们议论。凯氏的低年级生几乎是最晚听说的一拨，可一旦听说了，便把这事儿用了个十足——学校宿舍的跑腿生们对此大为光火，在他们看来，这桩事形同侵犯版权。当天因此爆发了好几场小型混战；那天晚上吃茶点时，凯氏餐厅餐桌的下端可以看到许多肿起来的脸。不过人人脸上都挂着得意的笑。他们向学校宿舍证明了自己有权拥有一起属于本宿舍的失窃案。这是自肯尼迪（Kennedy）出任舍长以来，凯氏宿舍头一回万众一心、共襄爱舍义举。

===Original===
Directly afternoon school was over that day, Fenn started for the town. The only thing that caused him any anxiety now was the fear lest the cap which he had left in the house in the High Street might rise up as evidence against him later on. Except for that, he was safe. The headmaster had evidently not remembered his absence from the festive board, or he would have spoken to him on the subject before now. If he could but recover the lost cap, all would be right with the world. Give him back that cap, and he would turn over a new leaf with a rapidity and emphasis which would lower the world’s record for that performance. He would be a reformed character.
